[Short-haul flight bans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Short-haul_flight_ban), train connections, personal choices and [taxation on flights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aviation_taxation_and_subsidies) can lead to fewer flights. [Hybrid electric aircraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hybrid_electric_aircraft) and [electric aircraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_aircraft) or [hydrogen-powered aircraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrogen-powered_aircraft) may replace fossil fuel-powered aircraft.[^cite_note-245]

Experts expect emissions from aviation to rise in most projections, at least until 2040. They currently amount to 180 Mt of CO<sub>2</sub> or 11% of transport emissions. [Aviation biofuel](17903761.md) and hydrogen can only cover a small proportion of flights in the coming years. Experts expect hybrid-driven aircraft to start commercial regional scheduled flights after 2030. Battery-powered aircraft are likely to enter the market after 2035.[^cite_note-246] Under CORSIA, flight operators can purchase [carbon offsets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_offset) to cover their emissions above 2019 levels. CORSIA will be compulsory from 2027.

### Agriculture, forestry and land use



[Greenhouse gas emissions](9528025.md) across the [supply chain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supply_chain) for different foods, showing which type of food should be encouraged and which discouraged from a mitigation perspective

See also: [Greenhouse gas emissions from agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenhouse_gas_emissions_from_agriculture), [Environmental impact of meat production](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_impact_of_meat_production), and [Sustainable agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sustainable_agriculture)

Almost 20% of greenhouse gas emissions come from the agriculture and forestry sector.[^cite_note-247] To significantly reduce these emissions, annual investments in the agriculture sector need to increase to \$260 billion by 2030. The potential benefits from these investments are estimated at \$4.3 trillion by 2030, offering a substantial economic return of 16-to-1.[^cite_note-248]<sup>: 7–8 </sup>

Mitigation measures in the food system can be divided into four categories. These are demand-side changes, ecosystem protections, mitigation on farms, and mitigation in [supply chains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supply_chains). On the demand side, limiting [food waste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_waste) is an effective way to reduce food emissions. Changes to a diet less reliant on animal products such as [plant-based diets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plant-based_diets) are also effective.[^cite_note-UNEP-2022-17]<sup>: XXV </sup>

With 21% of global methane emissions, cattle are a major driver of global warming.[^cite_note-Olivier-2020-249]<sup>: 6 </sup> When rainforests are cut and the land is converted for grazing, the impact is even higher. In Brazil, producing 1 kg of beef can result in the emission of up to 335 kg CO<sub>2</sub>-eq.[^cite_note-250] Increasing the milk yield of dairy cows has been shown to reduce emissions.[^cite_note-251] Other livestock, manure management and rice cultivation also emit greenhouse gases, in addition to fossil fuel combustion in agriculture.

Important mitigation options for reducing the greenhouse gas emissions from livestock include genetic selection,[^cite_note-252][^cite_note-253] introduction of [methanotrophic bacteria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methanotroph) into the rumen,[^cite_note-254][^cite_note-255] vaccines, feeds,[^cite_note-256] diet modification and grazing management.[^cite_note-257][^cite_note-258][^cite_note-259] Other options are diet changes towards [ruminant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruminant)-free alternatives, such as [milk substitutes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milk_substitute) and [meat analogues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meat_analogue). Non-ruminant livestock, such as poultry, emit far fewer GHGs.[^cite_note-260]

It is possible to cut methane emissions in rice cultivation by improved water management, combining dry seeding and one drawdown, or executing a [sequence of wetting and drying](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alternate_wetting_and_drying). This results in emission reductions of up to 90% compared to full flooding and even increased yields.[^cite_note-261]

Reducing the usage of [nitrogen fertilizers](37401.md#Nitrogen_fertilizers) through [nutrient management](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nutrient_management) could avoid nitrous oxide emissions equal to 2.77 - 11.48 gigatons of carbon dioxide from 2020 to 2050.[^cite_note-262]

### Industry

Global [carbon dioxide emissions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_carbon_dioxide_emissions) by country in 2023:

1. China (31.8%)

2. United States (14.4%)

3. European Union (4.90%)

4. India (9.50%)

5. Russia (5.80%)

6. Japan (3.50%)

7. Other (30.1%)

Industry is the largest emitter of greenhouse gases when direct and indirect emissions are included. [Electrification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electrification) can reduce emissions from industry. [Green hydrogen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_hydrogen) can play a major role in [energy-intensive industries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy-intensive_industries?action=edit\&redlink=1) for which electricity is not an option. Further mitigation options involve the steel and cement industry, which can switch to a less polluting production process. Products can be made with less material to reduce emission-intensity and industrial processes can be made more efficient. Finally, [circular economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circular_economy) measures reduce the need for new materials. This also saves on emissions that would have been released from the mining of collecting of those materials.[^cite_note-UNEP-2022-17]<sup>: 43 </sup>

The decarbonisation of cement production requires new technologies, and therefore investment in innovation.[^cite_note-IEA-2022-14] Bioconcrete is one possibility to reduce emissions.[^cite_note-263] But no technology for mitigation is yet mature. So CCS will be necessary at least in the short-term.[^cite_note-264]

Another sector with a significant carbon footprint is the steel sector, which is responsible for about 7% of global emissions.[^cite_note-Renewable_Reads-265] Emissions can be reduced by using [electric arc furnaces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_arc_furnaces) to melt and recycle scrap steel. To produce virgin steel without emissions, [blast furnaces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blast_furnace) could be replaced by hydrogen [direct reduced iron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direct_reduced_iron) and [electric arc furnaces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_arc_furnace). Alternatively, carbon capture and storage solutions can be used.[^cite_note-Renewable_Reads-265]

Coal, gas and oil production often come with significant methane leakage.[^cite_note-Krane-2022-266] In the early 2020s some governments recognised the scale of the problem and introduced regulations.[^cite_note-267] [Methane leaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methane_leaks) at oil and gas wells and processing plants are cost-effective to fix in countries which can easily trade gas internationally.[^cite_note-Krane-2022-266] There are leaks in countries where gas is cheap; such as Iran,[^cite_note-268] Russia,[^cite_note-269] and Turkmenistan.[^cite_note-270] Nearly all this can be stopped by replacing old components and preventing routine flaring.[^cite_note-Krane-2022-266] [Coalbed methane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalbed_methane) may continue leaking even after the mine has been closed. But it can be captured by drainage and/or ventilation systems.[^cite_note-271] Fossil fuel firms do not always have financial incentives to tackle methane leakage.[^cite_note-272]

## Co-benefits

Co-benefits of climate change mitigation, also often referred to as *ancillary benefits*, were firstly dominated in the scientific literature by studies that describe how lower GHG emissions lead to better air quality and consequently impact human health positively.[^cite_note-273][^cite_note-274] The scope of co-benefits research expanded to its economic, social, ecological and political implications.

Positive secondary effects that occur from climate mitigation and [adaptation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_adaptation) measures have been mentioned in research since the 1990s.[^cite_note-275][^cite_note-276] The IPCC first mentioned the role of co-benefits in 2001, followed by its fourth and fifth assessment cycle stressing improved working environment, reduced waste, health benefits and reduced capital expenditures.[^cite_note-277] In the early 2000s the [OECD](33853117.md) was further fostering its efforts in promoting ancillary benefits.[^cite_note-278]

The IPCC pointed out in 2007: "Co-benefits of GHG mitigation can be an important decision criteria in analyses carried out by policy-makers, but they are often neglected" and added that the co-benefits are "not quantified, monetised or even identified by businesses and decision-makers".[^cite_note-ipcc-279] Appropriate consideration of co-benefits can greatly "influence policy decisions concerning the timing and level of mitigation action", and there can be "significant advantages to the national economy and technical innovation".[^cite_note-ipcc-279]

An analysis of climate action in the UK found that public health benefits are a major component of the total benefits derived from climate action.[^cite_note-280]

### Employment and economic development

See also: [Renewable energy § Market and industry trends](25784.md#Market_and_industry_trends)

Co-benefits can positively impact employment, industrial development, states' energy independence and energy self-consumption. The deployment of renewable energies can foster job opportunities. Depending on the country and deployment scenario, replacing coal power plants with renewable energy can more than double the number of jobs per average MW capacity.[^cite_note-281] Investments in renewable energies, especially in solar- and wind energy, can boost the value of production.[^cite_note-282] Countries which rely on energy imports can enhance their energy independence and ensure supply security by deploying renewables. National energy generation from renewables lowers the demand for fossil fuel imports which scales up annual economic saving.[^cite_note-283]

The European Commission forecasts a shortage of 180,000 skilled workers in hydrogen production and 66,000 in solar photovoltaic power by 2030.[^cite_note-European_Investment_Bank-284]

### Energy security

A higher share of renewables can additionally lead to more [energy security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_security).[^cite_note-285][^cite_note-286] Socioeconomic co-benefits have been analysed such as energy access in rural areas and improved rural livelihoods.[^cite_note-IASS/TERI-2019-287][^cite_note-288] Rural areas which are not fully electrified can benefit from the deployment of [renewable energies](25784.md). Solar-powered mini-grids can remain economically viable, cost-competitive and reduce the number of power cuts. Energy reliability has additional social implications: stable electricity improves the quality of education.[^cite_note-IASS/TERI-2019-287]

The International Energy Agency ([IEA](285292.md)) spelled out the "multiple benefits approach" of [energy efficiency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Efficient_energy_use) while the International Renewable Energy Agency ([IRENA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Renewable_Energy_Agency)) operationalised the list of co-benefits of the renewable energy sector.[^cite_note-289][^cite_note-290]

### Health and well-being

Further information: [Effects of climate change on human health § Benefits from climate change mitigation and adaptation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effects_of_climate_change_on_human_health#Benefits_from_climate_change_mitigation_and_adaptation)

See also: [Health and environmental impact of the coal industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_and_environmental_impact_of_the_coal_industry) and [Health and environmental impact of the petroleum industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_and_environmental_impact_of_the_petroleum_industry)

The health benefits from climate change mitigation are significant. Potential measures can not only mitigate future health impacts from climate change but also improve health directly.[^cite_note-291][^cite_note-Molar-292] Climate change mitigation is interconnected with various health co-benefits, such as those from reduced [air pollution](10934212.md).[^cite_note-Molar-292] Air pollution generated by fossil fuel combustion is both a major driver of global warming and the cause of a large number of annual deaths. Some estimates are as high as 8.7 million excess deaths during 2018.[^cite_note-293][^cite_note-294] A 2023 study estimated that fossil fuels kill over 5 million people each year, as of 2019,[^cite_note-295] by causing diseases such as [heart attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heart_attack), [stroke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stroke) and [chronic obstructive pulmonary disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chronic_obstructive_pulmonary_disease).[^cite_note-296] [Particulate air pollution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Particulate_pollution) kills by far the most, followed by [ground-level ozone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ground-level_ozone).[^cite_note-297]

Mitigation policies can also promote healthier diets such as less red meat, more active lifestyles, and increased exposure to green urban spaces.[^cite_note-Romanello-2021-298][^cite_note-299] Access to urban green spaces provides benefits to mental health as well.[^cite_note-Romanello-2021-298]<sup>: 18 </sup> The increased use of [green](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_infrastructure) and [blue infrastructure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_space) can reduce the [urban heat island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_heat_island) effect. This reduces [heat stress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyperthermia) on people.[^cite_note-IPCC-2022-100]<sup>: TS-66 </sup>

### Climate change adaptation

Further information: [Climate change adaptation § Co-benefits with mitigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_adaptation#Co-benefits_with_mitigation)

Some mitigation measures have co-benefits in the area of [climate change adaptation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_adaptation).[^cite_note-AR6_WGIII_Ch_8-300]<sup>: 8–63 </sup> This is for example the case for many [nature-based solutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nature-based_solutions).[^cite_note-AR6_WGIII_Ch_4-301]<sup>: 4–94 </sup>[^cite_note-302]<sup>: 6 </sup> Examples in the urban context include urban green and blue infrastructure which provide mitigation as well as adaptation benefits. This can be in the form of [urban forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_forestry) and street trees, [green roofs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_roof) and [walls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_wall), [urban agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_agriculture) and so forth. The mitigation is achieved through the conservation and expansion of carbon sinks and reduced energy use of buildings. Adaptation benefits come for example through reduced heat stress and flooding risk.[^cite_note-AR6_WGIII_Ch_8-300]<sup>: 8–64 </sup>

Carbon taxes and emission trading worldwide

Emission trading and carbon taxes around the world (2019)

[^cite_note-303]

&#x20; [Carbon emission trading](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_emission_trading) implemented or scheduled

&#x20; [Carbon tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_tax) implemented or scheduled

&#x20; [Carbon emission trading](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_emission_trading) or [carbon tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_tax) under consideration

## Negative side effects

|  | This section **is written like a [personal reflection, personal essay, or argumentative essay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:What_Wikipedia_is_not#Wikipedia_is_not_a_publisher_of_original_thought)** that states a Wikipedia editor's personal feelings or presents an original argument about a topic. Please [help improve it](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Climate_change_mitigation\&action=edit) by rewriting it in an [encyclopedic style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Writing_better_articles#Information_style_and_tone). *(May 2026)*_&#x20;([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Maintenance_template_removal))_ |
| --- | --- |

Mitigation measures can also have negative side effects and risks.[^cite_note-IPCC-2022-100]<sup>: TS-133 </sup> In agriculture and forestry, mitigation measures can affect biodiversity and ecosystem functioning.[^cite_note-IPCC-2022-100]<sup>: TS-87 </sup> In renewable energy, mining for metals and minerals can increase threats to conservation areas.[^cite_note-304] There is some research into ways to recycle solar panels and electronic waste. This would create a source for materials so there is no need to mine them.[^cite_note-techrev-305][^cite_note-Lamb_et_al.-306]

Scholars have found that discussions about risks and negative side effects of mitigation measures can lead to deadlock or the feeling that there are insuperable barriers to taking action.[^cite_note-Lamb_et_al.-306] This deadlock or feelings often happens because people focus more on possible losses than benefits, making them hesitant to support change.[^cite_note-307] Uncertainty also plays a role, as decision-makers may overthink worst-case scenarios and delay action instead of moving forward.[^cite_note-308] In addition, strong disagreements between groups and the lack of a shared way to judge risks can make compromise difficult, leading to political gridlock.[^cite_note-309]

## Costs and funding

Main articles: [Economics of climate change mitigation § Assessing costs and benefits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economics_of_climate_change_mitigation#Assessing_costs_and_benefits), and [Economic analysis of climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_analysis_of_climate_change)

Several factors affect mitigation cost estimates. One is the baseline. This is a reference scenario that the alternative mitigation scenario is compared with. Others are the way costs are modelled, and assumptions about future government policy.[^cite_note-310]<sup>: 622 </sup> Cost estimates for mitigation for specific regions depend on the quantity of emissions allowed for that region in future, as well as the timing of interventions.[^cite_note-311]<sup>: 90 </sup>

Mitigation costs will vary according to how and when emissions are cut. Early, well-planned action will minimise the costs.[^cite_note-stern-155] Globally, the benefits of keeping warming under 2 °C exceed the costs,[^cite_note-Sampedro-2020-312] which according to [The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist) are affordable.[^cite_note-313]

Economists estimate the cost of climate change mitigation at between 1% and 2% of [GDP](12594.md).[^cite_note-Rhys-2019-314][^cite_note-Kotz2024-315] While this is a large sum, it is still far less than the subsidies governments provide to the fossil fuel industry. The [International Monetary Fund](15251.md) estimated this at more than \$5 trillion per year.[^cite_note-316][^cite_note-Teske-2019-50] A 2024 [opinion poll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opinion_poll) found 69% of the global population were willing to volunteer 1% of their income for climate change mitigation.[^cite_note-r865-317]

Another estimate says that financial flows for climate mitigation and adaptation are going to be over \$800 billion per year. These financial requirements are predicted to exceed \$4 trillion per year by 2030.[^cite_note-318][^cite_note-319]

Globally, limiting warming to 2 °C may result in higher economic benefits than economic costs.[^cite_note-320]<sup>: 300 </sup> The economic repercussions of mitigation vary widely across regions and households, depending on policy design and level of [international cooperation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_cooperation). Delayed global cooperation increases policy costs across regions, especially in those that are relatively carbon intensive at present. Pathways with uniform carbon values show higher mitigation costs in more carbon-intensive regions, in fossil-fuels exporting regions and in poorer regions. Aggregate quantifications expressed in GDP or monetary terms undervalue the economic effects on households in poorer countries. The actual effects on welfare and well-being are comparatively larger.[^cite_note-AR6_WGIII_Ch_3-321]

[Cost–benefit analysis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cost%E2%80%93benefit_analysis) may be unsuitable for analysing climate change mitigation as a whole. But it is still useful for analysing the difference between a 1.5 °C target and 2 °C.[^cite_note-Rhys-2019-314] One way of estimating the cost of reducing emissions is by considering the likely costs of potential technological and output changes. Policymakers can compare the [marginal abatement costs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marginal_abatement_costs) of different methods to assess the cost and amount of possible abatement over time. The marginal abatement costs of the various measures will differ by country, by sector, and over time.[^cite_note-stern-155]

[Eco-tariffs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eco-tariff) on only imports contribute to reduced global export [competitiveness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Competition_\(economics\)) and to [deindustrialisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deindustrialisation).[^cite_note-o344-322]

### Avoided costs of climate change effects

See also: [Economic impacts of climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_impacts_of_climate_change)

It is possible to avoid some of the costs of the [effects of climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effects_of_climate_change) by limiting climate change. According to the [Stern Review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stern_Review), inaction can be as high as the equivalent of losing at least 5% of global gross domestic product (GDP) each year, now and forever. This can be up to 20% of GDP or more when including a wider range of risks and impacts. But mitigating climate change will only cost about 2% of GDP. Also it may not be a good idea from a financial perspective to delay significant reductions in greenhouse gas emissions.[^cite_note-323][^cite_note-324]

Mitigation solutions are often evaluated in terms of costs and greenhouse gas reduction potentials. This fails to take into account the direct effects on human well-being.[^cite_note-325]

### Distributing emissions abatement costs

Mitigation at the speed and scale required to limit warming to 2 °C or below implies deep economic and structural changes. These raise multiple types of distributional concerns across regions, income classes and sectors.[^cite_note-AR6_WGIII_Ch_3-321]

There have been different proposals on how to allocate responsibility for cutting emissions.[^cite_note-banuri-326]<sup>: 103 </sup> These include [egalitarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egalitarianism), [basic needs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basic_needs) according to a minimum level of consumption, proportionality and the [polluter-pays principle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polluter_pays_principle). A specific proposal is "equal per capita entitlements".[^cite_note-banuri-326]<sup>: 106 </sup> This approach has two categories. In the first category, emissions are allocated according to national population. In the second category, emissions are allocated in a way that attempts to account for historical or cumulative emissions.

### Funding

Main articles: [Climate finance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_finance) and [Economics of climate change mitigation § Finance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economics_of_climate_change_mitigation#Finance)

In order to reconcile economic development with mitigating carbon emissions, developing countries need particular support. This would be both financial and technical. The IPCC found that accelerated support would also tackle inequities in financial and economic vulnerability to climate change.[^cite_note-327] One way to achieve this is the Kyoto Protocol's [Clean Development Mechanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clean_Development_Mechanism) (CDM).

## Policies

### National policies



Although China is the leading producer of CO

2

&#x20;emissions in the world with the U.S. second, per capita the U.S. leads China by a fair margin (data from 2017).

Climate change mitigation policies can have a large and complex impact on the socio-economic status of individuals and countries. This can be both positive and negative.[^cite_note-328] It is important to design policies well and make them inclusive. Otherwise climate change mitigation measures can impose higher financial costs on poor households.[^cite_note-329] Current climate policies contribute to some changes but remain far from accelerating low-carbon transitions beyond historical trends and to levels consistent with Paris climate targets,[^cite_note-Suzuki2023-5] and would still result in global warming of about 2.7 °C by 2100.[^cite_note-Ritchie2022-6]

In order to identify relatively more effective policies, an evaluation was conducted on 1,500 climate policy interventions made between 1998 and 2022.[^cite_note-Stechemesser2024-4] The interventions took place in 41 countries and across 6 continents, which together contributed 81% of the world's total emissions as of 2019. The evaluation found 63 successful interventions that resulted in significant emission reductions; the total CO<sub>2</sub> release averted by these interventions was between 0.6 and 1.8 billion metric tonnes. The study focused on interventions with at least 4.5% emission reductions, but the researchers noted that meeting the reductions required by the Paris Agreement would require 23 billion metric tonnes per year. Generally, carbon pricing was found to be most effective in [developed countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developed_country), while regulation was most effective in the [developing countries](78449.md). Complementary policy mixes benefited from synergies, and were mostly found to be more effective interventions than the implementation of isolated policies.[^cite_note-330][^cite_note-331][^cite_note-The_Boston_Globe_9-4-2024-332]

The [OECD](33853117.md) recognise 48 distinct climate mitigation policies suitable for implementation at national level. Broadly, these can be categorised into three types: *market based* instruments, *non market based* instruments and *other* policies.[^cite_note-capmf-333][^cite_note-Stechemesser2024-4]

- **Other** policies include the *Establishing an Independent climate advisory body*.

  [^cite_note-capmf-333]

- **Non market based** policies include the Implementing or tightening of *Regulatory standards*. These set technology or performance standards. They can be effective in addressing the [market failure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_failure) of informational barriers.

  [^cite_note-bashmakov3-334]

  : 412&#x20;

- Among **market based** policies, the *carbon price* has been found to be the most effective (at least for developed economies),

  [^cite_note-Stechemesser2024-4]

  &#x20;and has its own section below. Additional *market based* policy instruments for climate change mitigation include:

*Emissions taxes* These often require domestic emitters to pay a fixed fee or tax for every tonne of CO<sub>2</sub> emissions they release into the atmosphere.[^cite_note-bashmakov3-334]<sup>: 4123 </sup> [Methane emissions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methane_emissions) from fossil fuel extraction are also occasionally taxed.[^cite_note-335] But methane and nitrous oxide from agriculture are typically not subject to tax.[^cite_note-336] \
*Removing unhelpful subsidies:* Many countries provide subsidies for activities that affect emissions. For example, significant [fossil fuel subsidies](44334234.md) are present in many countries.[^cite_note-337] [Phasing-out fossil fuel subsidies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fossil_fuel_phase-out#Phase-out_of_fossil_fuel_subsidies) is crucial to address the climate crisis.[^cite_note-338] It must however be done carefully to avoid protests[^cite_note-339] and making poor people poorer.[^cite_note-340] \
*Creating helpful subsidies*: Creating subsidies and financial incentives.[^cite_note-341] One example is [energy subsidies](16174922.md) to support clean generation which is not yet commercially viable such as tidal power.[^cite_note-342] \
*Tradable permits*: A [permit system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_emission_trading) can limit emissions.[^cite_note-bashmakov3-334]<sup>: 415 </sup>

#### Carbon pricing

Main article: [Carbon price](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_price)



Carbon emission trade – allowance prices from 2008

Imposing additional costs on greenhouse gas emissions can make fossil fuels less competitive and accelerate investments into low-carbon sources of energy. A growing number of countries raise a fixed [carbon tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_tax) or participate in dynamic [carbon emission trading](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_emission_trading) (ETS) systems. In 2021, more than 21% of global greenhouse gas emissions were covered by a carbon price. This was a big increase from earlier due to the introduction of the [Chinese national carbon trading scheme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_national_carbon_trading_scheme).[^cite_note-343]<sup>: 23 </sup>

Trading schemes offer the possibility to limit emission allowances to certain reduction targets. However, an oversupply of allowances keeps most ETS at low price levels around \$10 with a low impact. This includes the Chinese ETS which started with \$7/tCO<sub>2</sub> in 2021.[^cite_note-344] One exception is the [European Union Emission Trading Scheme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Union_Emission_Trading_Scheme) where prices began to rise in 2018. They reached about €80/tCO<sub>2</sub> in 2022.[^cite_note-345] This results in additional costs of about €0.04/KWh for coal and €0.02/KWh for gas combustion for electricity, depending on the [emission intensity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emission_intensity).[^cite_note-346] Industries which have high energy requirements and high emissions often pay only very low energy taxes, or even none at all.[^cite_note-AR6_WGIII_Ch_11-347]<sup>: 11–80 </sup>

While this is often part of national schemes, [carbon offsets and credits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_offsets_and_credits) can be part of a voluntary market as well such as on the international market. Notably, the company [Blue Carbon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Carbon_\(company\)) of the [UAE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UAE) has bought ownership over an area equivalent to the United Kingdom to be preserved in return for carbon credits.[^cite_note-348]

### International agreements

Main article: [Politics of climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_climate_change)

See also: [Climate change § Policies and politics](5042951.md#Policies_and_politics), and [Climate change mitigation framework](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_mitigation_framework)

[International cooperation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_cooperation) is considered a *critical enabler* for climate action[^cite_note-AR6_WGIII_Ch_13-11]<sup>: 52 </sup> while conflicts generally hamper it.[^cite_note-349] Almost all countries are parties to the [United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change](31898.md) (UNFCCC).[^cite_note-350][^cite_note-351] The ultimate objective of the UNFCCC is to stabilise atmospheric concentrations of greenhouse gases at a level that would prevent dangerous human interference with the climate system.[^cite_note-352]

Although not designed for this purpose, the [Montreal Protocol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montreal_Protocol) has benefited climate change mitigation efforts.[^cite_note-353] The Montreal Protocol is an international treaty that has successfully reduced emissions of [ozone-depleting substances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ozone-depleting_substance) such as [CFCs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chlorofluorocarbon). These are also greenhouse gases.

#### Supply-side vs. demand-side policies

While international climate efforts have traditionally focused on demand-side policies like carbon taxes and emissions trading, a growing body of research explores supply-side treaties aimed at reducing the global output of fossil fuels. Proponents argue these treaties may be more cost-efficient and easier to monitor than demand-side agreements, potentially addressing issues like 'carbon leakage' and the 'green paradox'.[^cite_note-Stankovic-2024-354] However, scholars note that because most major emitters are also major producers, supply-side treaties face similar challenges in participation and distributive conflict.[^cite_note-Stankovic-2024-354]

## History

See also: [Climate change mitigation framework](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_mitigation_framework), [History of climate change policy and politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_climate_change_policy_and_politics), [Kyoto Protocol § Chronology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyoto_Protocol#Chronology), and [Paris Agreement § Development](30242372.md#Development)

Historically efforts to deal with climate change have taken place at a multinational level. They involve attempts to reach a consensus decision at the United Nations, under the [United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change](31898.md) (UNFCCC).[^cite_note-355] This is the dominant approach historically of engaging as many international governments as possible in taking action on a worldwide public issue. The [Montreal Protocol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montreal_Protocol) in 1987 is a precedent that this approach can work. But some critics say the top-down framework of only utilising the UNFCCC consensus approach is ineffective. They put forward counter-proposals of bottom-up governance. At this same time this would lessen the emphasis on the UNFCCC.[^cite_note-Cole-2015-356][^cite_note-Sabel-2017-357][^cite_note-Zefferman-2018-358]

The [Kyoto Protocol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyoto_Protocol) to the UNFCCC adopted in 1997 set out legally binding emission reduction commitments for the "Annex 1" countries.[^cite_note-359]<sup>: 817 </sup> The Protocol defined three international policy instruments ("[Flexibility Mechanisms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flexibility_mechanisms)") which could be used by the Annex 1 countries to meet their emission reduction commitments. According to Bashmakov, use of these instruments could significantly reduce the costs for Annex 1 countries in meeting their emission reduction commitments.[^cite_note-bashmakov-360]<sup>: 402 </sup>

The Paris Agreement reached in 2015 succeeded the [Kyoto Protocol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyoto_Protocol) which expired in 2020. [Countries that ratified the Kyoto protocol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Kyoto_Protocol_signatories) committed to reduce their emissions of carbon dioxide and five other greenhouse gases, or engage in [carbon emissions trading](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_emissions_trading) if they maintain or increase emissions of these gases.

In 2015, the UNFCCC's "structured expert dialogue" came to the conclusion that, "in some regions and vulnerable ecosystems, high risks are projected even for warming above 1.5 °C".[^cite_note-361] Together with the strong diplomatic voice of the poorest countries and the island nations in the Pacific, this expert finding was the driving force leading to the decision of the 2015 [Paris Climate Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_United_Nations_Climate_Change_Conference) to lay down this 1.5 °C long-term target on top of the existing 2 °C goal.[^cite_note-362]

## Barriers

See also: [Economic analysis of climate change § Economic barriers to addressing climate change mitigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_analysis_of_climate_change#Economic_barriers_to_addressing_climate_change_mitigation), [Climate change denial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_denial), [Public opinion on climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_opinion_on_climate_change), and [Sustainability § Barriers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sustainability#Barriers)



A typology of discourses aimed at delaying climate change mitigation

[^cite_note-Lamb_et_al.-306]



Distribution of committed CO

2

&#x20;emissions from developed fossil fuel reserves

There are individual, institutional and market barriers to achieving climate change mitigation.[^cite_note-AR6_WGIII_Ch_5-101]<sup>: 5–71 </sup> They differ for all the different mitigation options, regions and societies.

Difficulties with [accounting for carbon dioxide removal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_accounting) can act as economic barriers. This would apply to BECCS ([bioenergy with carbon capture and storage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bioenergy_with_carbon_capture_and_storage)).[^cite_note-AR6_WGIII_Ch_6-49]<sup>: 6–42 </sup>

For land-based mitigation options, finance is a major barrier. Other barriers are cultural values, governance, accountability and institutional capacity.[^cite_note-AR6_WGIII_Ch_7-131]<sup>: 7–5 </sup>

Developing countries face further barriers to mitigation:[^cite_note-363]

- The cost of capital increased in the early 2020s.

  [^cite_note-364]

  &#x20;A lack of available capital and finance is common in developing countries.

  [^cite_note-365]

  &#x20;Together with the absence of regulatory standards, this barrier supports the proliferation of inefficient equipment.

- There are also financial and [capacity](3045799.md) barrier in many of these countries.

  [^cite_note-AR6_WGIII_Ch_5-101]

  : 97&#x20;

One study estimates that only 0.12% of all funding for climate-related research goes on the social science of climate change mitigation.[^cite_note-Overland-2020-366] Vastly more funding goes on natural science studies of climate change. Considerable sums also go on studies of the impact of climate change and adaptation to it.[^cite_note-Overland-2020-366]

## Society and culture

### Commitments to divest



More firms plan to invest in climate change mitigation, specifically focusing on low-carbon sectors.

[^cite_note-367]

More than 1000 organisations with investments worth US\$8 trillion have made commitments to [fossil fuel divestment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fossil_fuel_divestment).[^cite_note-368] Socially responsible investing funds allow investors to invest in funds that meet high [environmental, social and corporate governance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental,_social_and_corporate_governance) (ESG) standards.[^cite_note-369]

### Impacts of the COVID-19 pandemic

Main article: [Impact of the COVID-19 pandemic on the environment § Climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_on_the_environment#Climate_change)

The [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md) led some governments to shift their focus away from climate action, at least temporarily.[^cite_note-370] This obstacle to environmental policy efforts may have contributed to slowed investment in green energy technologies. The economic slowdown resulting from COVID-19 added to this effect.[^cite_note-UN-371][^cite_note-9mMFi-372]

In 2020, carbon dioxide emissions fell by 6.4% or 2.3 billion tonnes globally.[^cite_note-Tollefson-2021-373] Greenhouse gas emissions rebounded later in the pandemic as many countries began lifting restrictions. The direct impact of pandemic policies had a negligible long-term impact on climate change.[^cite_note-Tollefson-2021-373][^cite_note-effectspaper-374]

## Examples by country



Greenhouse gas emissions *per person* in the highest-emitting countries.

[^cite_note-GlobalCarbonAtlas_Territorial_MtCO2-375]

&#x20;Though China has the greatest total annual carbon dioxide emissions, the U.S. and a few other high-emitting countries exceed China in *per capita* emissions.



Richer [(developed)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developed_country) countries emit more CO

2

&#x20;per person than poorer [(developing)](78449.md) countries.

[^cite_note-WashPost_20230301-376]

&#x20;Emissions are roughly proportional to [GDP](12594.md) per person, though the rate of increase diminishes with average GDP/pp of about \$10,000.

While climate mitigation is often cast as a burden to be distributed in some or other way, it can also be seen as an opportunity for industrial development, exports, and status seeking for countries.[^cite_note-Overland-2024-19] The examples and precedents that countries set may influence other countries to follow suit.[^cite_note-Overland-2024-19]

### United States

Main article: [Climate change in the United States](18246585.md)

This section is an excerpt from [Greenhouse gas emissions by the United States § Federal Policies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenhouse_gas_emissions_by_the_United_States#Federal_Policies).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Greenhouse_gas_emissions_by_the_United_States\&action=edit)]

The United States government has held shifting attitudes toward addressing greenhouse gas emissions. The [George W. Bush administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_George_W._Bush) opted not to sign the [Kyoto Protocol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyoto_Protocol),[^cite_note-Greenhouse_gas_emissions_by_the_United_States_Dessai_2001_5-377] but the [Obama administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obama_administration) entered the [Paris Agreement](30242372.md).[^cite_note-378] The [Trump administration](52231341.md) withdrew from the [Paris Agreement](30242372.md) while increasing the export of [crude oil](23195.md) and [gas](23639.md), making the [United States](3434750.md) the largest producer.[^cite_note-379]

In 2021, the [Biden administration](65732174.md) committed to reducing emissions to half of 2005 levels by 2030.[^cite_note-380] In 2022, [President Biden](145422.md) signed the [Inflation Reduction Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inflation_Reduction_Act_of_2022) into law, which is estimated to provide around \$375 billion over 10 years to fight climate change.[^cite_note-381] As of 2022 the [social cost of carbon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_cost_of_carbon) is 51 dollars a tonne whereas academics say it should be more than three times higher.[^cite_note-382]

### China

Main articles: [Greenhouse gas emissions by China](61886740.md), [Climate change in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_China), and [Debate over China's economic responsibilities for climate change mitigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Debate_over_China's_economic_responsibilities_for_climate_change_mitigation)

China has committed to peak emissions by 2030 and reach [net zero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Net_zero) by 2060.[^cite_note-383] Warming cannot be limited to 1.5 °C if any [coal plants in China](20184277.md#Coal_power) (without carbon capture) operate after 2045.[^cite_note-384] The [Chinese national carbon trading scheme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_national_carbon_trading_scheme) started in 2021.

### European Union

The [European Commission](9974.md) estimates that an additional €477 million in annual investment is needed for the European Union to meet its [Fit-for-55](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fit_for_55) decarbonisation goals.[^cite_note-European_Investment_Bank-284][^cite_note-385]

In the European Union, government-driven policies and the [European Green Deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Green_Deal) have helped position greentech (as an example) as a vital area for venture capital investment. By 2023, venture capital in the EU's greentech sector equalled that of the United States, reflecting a concerted effort to drive innovation and mitigate climate change through targeted financial support.[^cite_note-European_Investment_Bank-284][^cite_note-386] The European Green Deal has fostered policies that contributed to a 30% rise in venture capital for greentech companies in the EU from 2021 to 2023, despite a downturn in other sectors during the same period.\[^cite_note-[www.cleantechforeurope.com-387](http://www.cleantechforeurope.com-387/)]

While overall venture capital investment in the EU remains about six times lower than in the United States, the greentech sector has closed this gap significantly, attracting substantial funding. Key areas benefitting from increased investments are energy storage, circular economy initiatives, and agricultural technology. This is supported by the EU's ambitious goal to reduce greenhouse gas emissions by at least 55% by 2030.\[^cite_note-[www.cleantechforeurope.com-387](http://www.cleantechforeurope.com-387/)]

## Related approaches

### Relationship with solar radiation modification (SRM)

While [solar radiation modification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_geoengineering) (SRM) could reduce surface temperatures, it temporarily masks climate change rather than addressing the root cause, which is greenhouse gases.[^cite_note-AR6_WGIII_Ch_14-388]<sup>: 14–56 </sup> SRM would work by altering how much solar radiation the Earth absorbs.[^cite_note-AR6_WGIII_Ch_14-388]<sup>: 14–56 </sup> Examples include reducing the amount of sunlight reaching the surface, reducing the optical thickness and lifetime of clouds, and changing the ability of the surface to reflect radiation.[^cite_note-389] The [IPCC](15030.md) describes SRM as a climate risk reduction strategy or supplementary option rather than a climate mitigation option.[^cite_note-AR6_WGIII_Ch_14-388]

The terminology in this area is still evolving. Experts sometimes use the term *geoengineering* or [climate engineering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_engineering) in the scientific literature for both CDR or SRM, if the techniques are used at a global scale.[^cite_note-AR6_WGIII_Ch_1-20]<sup>: 6–11 </sup> IPCC reports no longer use the terms *geoengineering* or *climate engineering*.[^cite_note-IPCC_AR6_WGI_Glossary-21]

[Bibcode](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bibcode_\(identifier\)):[2022RvEEP..16..219F](https://ui.adsabs.harvard.edu/abs/2022RvEEP..16..219F). [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1086/721078](https://doi.org/10.1086%2F721078). [S2CID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S2CID_\(identifier\)) [250721559](https://api.semanticscholar.org/CorpusID\:250721559). animal-based agriculture and feed crop production account for approximately 83 percent of agricultural land globally and are responsible for approximately 67 percent of deforestation (Poore and Nemecek 2018). This makes livestock farming the single largest driver of greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions, nutrient pollution, and ecosystem loss in the agricultural sector. A failure to mitigate GHG emissions from the food system, especially animal-based agriculture, could prevent the world from meeting the climate objective of limiting global warming to 1.5°C, as set forth in the Paris Climate Agreement, and complicate the path to limiting climate change to well below 2°C of warming (Clark et al. 2020).

[doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1098/rsfs.2019.0129](https://doi.org/10.1098%2Frsfs.2019.0129). [PMID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PMID_\(identifier\)) [32832065](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32832065). Analysis of wetland restoration costs relative to the amount of carbon they can sequester revealed that restoration is more cost-effective in coastal wetlands such as mangroves (US\$1800 ton C−1) compared with inland wetlands (US\$4200–49 200 ton C−1). We advise that for inland wetlands, priority should be given to conservation rather than restoration; while for coastal wetlands, both conservation and restoration may be effective techniques for climate change mitigation.
